Um… yeah. I guess I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially after work. I’m a cashier, so after a long shift I’ll leave and instead of just going home, I’ll stop and buy these huge takeout orders. Like, not just a meal — it’ll be enough for a whole family, honestly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I mean, I guess the honest answer is no, I don’t feel in control. It’s like I might be thinking clearly right before, like, “Okay, just eat some of it, don’t go crazy,” but once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off.

But my hands just keep going back for the next thing — another fry, another piece of chicken, opening another container, taking another bite — and it feels like I’m watching myself do it but not really stopping it.

It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. At first maybe, but then it’s more like I’m just… locked into it. I keep eating even when that part of me knows I should stop.

It feels embarrassing to even say it out loud, because it’s just… a lot of food. Way more than I think one person should be eating in one sitting. I keep telling myself I’m not going to do it again, but after my shifts it keeps happening, and that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control while eating these large amounts of food after work?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I guess the honest answer is no, I don’t feel in control. It’s like I might be thinking clearly right before, like, “Okay, just eat some of it, don’t go crazy,” but once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already ate enough.” Like I’m actually saying it in my head, sometimes over and over. But my hands just keep going back for the next thing — another fry, another piece of chicken, opening another container, taking another bite — and it feels like I’m watching myself do it but not really stopping it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. At first maybe, but then it’s more like I’m just… locked into it. I keep eating even when that part of me knows I should stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I feel bad afterward, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.

Sometimes it’s more like I’m just sitting there with all the wrappers and containers around me and I feel kind of… blank, or embarrassed, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t always have a clear thought like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this heavy feeling, like I don’t want anybody to know what just happened.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, that happens. I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because it’s not even like I’m still eating because it tastes amazing the whole time.

At the beginning, sure, it’s like I really want it, and the first bites feel like this relief almost. But then after a while, I’m not even really tasting it the same way. It’s just chewing and reaching and opening the next container. I’ll have this thought in my head like, Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough. And I really do mean it when I think that.

But once I’ve started, once I take that first bite, it feels like something in me clicks off. Like the part of me that can make a normal decision just kind of… shuts down. My hands keep moving before I can stop them. I’ll be telling myself to stop while I’m still grabbing another fry or another piece of food. So yeah, even when it’s not tasting good anymore, I keep going.

I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… it feels fast, but I don’t know if I’ve ever really timed it or paid attention in that specific way.

I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.

So… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.

I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.

Compared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.

So… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.

Burgers, fries, chicken, desserts, sometimes a mix from different places if I’ve got it in my head.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there and eat almost all of it by myself before I go home. It feels embarrassing to even say it out loud, because it’s just… a lot of food. Way more than I think one person should be eating in one sitting. I keep telling myself I’m not going to do it again, but after my shifts it keeps happening, and that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control while eating these large amounts of food after work?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I guess the honest answer is no, I don’t feel in control. It’s like I might be thinking clearly right before, like, “Okay, just eat some of it, don’t go crazy,” but once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already ate enough.” Like I’m actually saying it in my head, sometimes over and over. But my hands just keep going back for the next thing — another fry, another piece of chicken, opening another container, taking another bite — and it feels like I’m watching myself do it but not really stopping it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. At first maybe, but then it’s more like I’m just… locked into it. I keep eating even when that part of me knows I should stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I feel bad afterward, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes it’s more like I’m just sitting there with all the wrappers and containers around me and I feel kind of… blank, or embarrassed, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t always have a clear thought like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this heavy feeling, like I don’t want anybody to know what just happened.\n\nSo yeah, maybe guilt is part of it, but I guess I don’t really have a clean answer. It’s not something I’ve put words to very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a point during these episodes where the food doesn't even taste good to you anymore, but you continue eating anyway?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because it’s not even like I’m still eating because it tastes amazing the whole time.\n\nAt the beginning, sure, it’s like I really want it, and the first bites feel like this relief almost. But then after a while, I’m not even really tasting it the same way. It’s just chewing and reaching and opening the next container. I’ll have this thought in my head like, Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough. And I really do mean it when I think that.\n\nBut once I’ve started, once I take that first bite, it feels like something in me clicks off. Like the part of me that can make a normal decision just kind of… shuts down. My hands keep moving before I can stop them. I’ll be telling myself to stop while I’m still grabbing another fry or another piece of food. So yeah, even when it’s not tasting good anymore, I keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… it feels fast, but I don’t know if I’ve ever really timed it or paid attention in that specific way.\n\nWhen it’s happening, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m eating quickly.” I’m more just kind of in it, you know? Like I’ll look down and a lot of the food is gone sooner than I expected, but I don’t always know if that means I was eating really fast or if I just wasn’t paying attention.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t want to say yes like I know for sure. I just know it can feel like everything kind of happens in a blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.\n\nMy clothes fit differently sometimes, or I’ll feel heavier or bloated after certain periods, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain over time or just how I’m feeling in my body. And I don’t really have a clear before-and-after number I can give you.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are eating more quickly than you normally would during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.\n\nCompared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not sure it’s specifically after work, or specifically because I’m stressed or anxious. I mean, work can definitely leave me feeling worn out sometimes, just from standing there and dealing with people all day, but I don’t know if I can honestly connect it in a straight line like, “I had a bad shift, so then I ate a large amount.”

Sometimes by the time I get home, I’m just kind of drained and not really thinking clearly, and eating can sort of happen before I’ve even sorted out what I’m feeling. But I don’t always know if I’m upset first, or if I’m just tired, or if it’s more like I’m alone and the day is over and there’s nothing else I have to focus on.

So… I guess I can’t say usually, no. Not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. It might happen around those feelings sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say it’s mostly stress or anxiety after work.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I guess I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially after work. I’m a cashier, so after a long shift I’ll leave and instead of just going home, I’ll stop and buy these huge takeout orders. Like, not just a meal — it’ll be enough for a whole family, honestly. Burgers, fries, chicken, desserts, sometimes a mix from different places if I’ve got it in my head.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there and eat almost all of it by myself before I go home. It feels embarrassing to even say it out loud, because it’s just… a lot of food. Way more than I think one person should be eating in one sitting. I keep telling myself I’m not going to do it again, but after my shifts it keeps happening, and that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control while eating these large amounts of food after work?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I guess the honest answer is no, I don’t feel in control. It’s like I might be thinking clearly right before, like, “Okay, just eat some of it, don’t go crazy,” but once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already ate enough.” Like I’m actually saying it in my head, sometimes over and over. But my hands just keep going back for the next thing — another fry, another piece of chicken, opening another container, taking another bite — and it feels like I’m watching myself do it but not really stopping it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. At first maybe, but then it’s more like I’m just… locked into it. I keep eating even when that part of me knows I should stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I feel bad afterward, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes it’s more like I’m just sitting there with all the wrappers and containers around me and I feel kind of… blank, or embarrassed, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t always have a clear thought like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this heavy feeling, like I don’t want anybody to know what just happened.\n\nSo yeah, maybe guilt is part of it, but I guess I don’t really have a clean answer. It’s not something I’ve put words to very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a point during these episodes where the food doesn't even taste good to you anymore, but you continue eating anyway?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because it’s not even like I’m still eating because it tastes amazing the whole time.\n\nAt the beginning, sure, it’s like I really want it, and the first bites feel like this relief almost. But then after a while, I’m not even really tasting it the same way. It’s just chewing and reaching and opening the next container. I’ll have this thought in my head like, Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough. And I really do mean it when I think that.\n\nBut once I’ve started, once I take that first bite, it feels like something in me clicks off. Like the part of me that can make a normal decision just kind of… shuts down. My hands keep moving before I can stop them. I’ll be telling myself to stop while I’m still grabbing another fry or another piece of food. So yeah, even when it’s not tasting good anymore, I keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… it feels fast, but I don’t know if I’ve ever really timed it or paid attention in that specific way.\n\nWhen it’s happening, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m eating quickly.” I’m more just kind of in it, you know? Like I’ll look down and a lot of the food is gone sooner than I expected, but I don’t always know if that means I was eating really fast or if I just wasn’t paying attention.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t want to say yes like I know for sure. I just know it can feel like everything kind of happens in a blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.\n\nMy clothes fit differently sometimes, or I’ll feel heavier or bloated after certain periods, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain over time or just how I’m feeling in my body. And I don’t really have a clear before-and-after number I can give you.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are eating more quickly than you normally would during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.\n\nCompared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food in response to feeling stressed, anxious, or upset after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically after work, or specifically because I’m stressed or anxious. I mean, work can definitely leave me feeling worn out sometimes, just from standing there and dealing with people all day, but I don’t know if I can honestly connect it in a straight line like, “I had a bad shift, so then I ate a large amount.”\n\nSometimes by the time I get home, I’m just kind of drained and not really thinking clearly, and eating can sort of happen before I’ve even sorted out what I’m feeling. But I don’t always know if I’m upset first, or if I’m just tired, or if it’s more like I’m alone and the day is over and there’s nothing else I have to focus on.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say usually, no. Not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. It might happen around those feelings sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say it’s mostly stress or anxiety after work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah… that part I can say more clearly. Once I start, it feels like I can’t really stop, even when part of me is trying to.

It’s like once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off. I’ll be thinking, like, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. I don’t know if I’d say “immediately” every time. That word makes me hesitate, because sometimes right after it happens I’m kind of numb, like I’m just sitting there and realizing what I did, but I’m not even fully feeling it yet. It’s more like this blank feeling at first, like… okay, that just happened.

Then the guilt can come in after, but I’m not sure exactly how fast. Sometimes it’s pretty soon, like once I see the wrappers or the empty containers or I notice how much is gone, then I start feeling really bad about myself. But other times I think I’m more dazed than guilty at first, if that makes sense.

So I guess I can’t honestly say I usually feel guilty immediately. I do feel bad afterward a lot of the time, but the timing is kind of messy. It’s not always like the second I stop eating I’m hit with guilt. Sometimes it takes a little while for my head to catch up.

I just know it can feel like everything kind of happens in a blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.\n\nMy clothes fit differently sometimes, or I’ll feel heavier or bloated after certain periods, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain over time or just how I’m feeling in my body. And I don’t really have a clear before-and-after number I can give you.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are eating more quickly than you normally would during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.\n\nCompared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food in response to feeling stressed, anxious, or upset after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically after work, or specifically because I’m stressed or anxious. I mean, work can definitely leave me feeling worn out sometimes, just from standing there and dealing with people all day, but I don’t know if I can honestly connect it in a straight line like, “I had a bad shift, so then I ate a large amount.”\n\nSometimes by the time I get home, I’m just kind of drained and not really thinking clearly, and eating can sort of happen before I’ve even sorted out what I’m feeling. But I don’t always know if I’m upset first, or if I’m just tired, or if it’s more like I’m alone and the day is over and there’s nothing else I have to focus on.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say usually, no. Not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. It might happen around those feelings sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say it’s mostly stress or anxiety after work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I can say more clearly. Once I start, it feels like I can’t really stop, even when part of me is trying to.\n\nIt’s like once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off. I’ll be thinking, like, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough.” I’ll actually be saying that in my head, not just afterward but while it’s happening. And then my hands still keep reaching for the next thing anyway, like I’m watching myself do it but not really controlling it.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I want more food now.” It feels more automatic than that. I can know I should stop and still keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty immediately after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “immediately” every time. That word makes me hesitate, because sometimes right after it happens I’m kind of numb, like I’m just sitting there and realizing what I did, but I’m not even fully feeling it yet. It’s more like this blank feeling at first, like… okay, that just happened.\n\nThen the guilt can come in after, but I’m not sure exactly how fast. Sometimes it’s pretty soon, like once I see the wrappers or the empty containers or I notice how much is gone, then I start feeling really bad about myself. But other times I think I’m more dazed than guilty at first, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I can’t honestly say I usually feel guilty immediately. I do feel bad afterward a lot of the time, but the timing is kind of messy. It’s not always like the second I stop eating I’m hit with guilt. Sometimes it takes a little while for my head to catch up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… yes. That’s probably the clearest way to describe it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, it’s that once I’ve started, it feels like I don’t have control of it anymore.

Like, once I take that first bite, something in me kind of clicks off. I can still hear myself thinking, you know, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. This is enough.” I’ll be telling myself that while it’s happening, not just afterward when I’m looking back on it. But even with that thought going through my head, my hands just keep reaching for the next thing, and then the next thing.

It’s a really strange feeling, because part of me is there and knows I want to stop, but another part is just… doing it anyway. It doesn’t feel like a normal choice in that moment. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though I already decided I should stop.

I keep telling myself I’m not going to do it again, but after my shifts it keeps happening, and that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control while eating these large amounts of food after work?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I guess the honest answer is no, I don’t feel in control. It’s not something I’ve put words to very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a point during these episodes where the food doesn't even taste good to you anymore, but you continue eating anyway?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because it’s not even like I’m still eating because it tastes amazing the whole time.\n\nAt the beginning, sure, it’s like I really want it, and the first bites feel like this relief almost. But then after a while, I’m not even really tasting it the same way. It’s just chewing and reaching and opening the next container. I’ll have this thought in my head like, Demetrius, stop. I’ll be telling myself to stop while I’m still grabbing another fry or another piece of food. So yeah, even when it’s not tasting good anymore, I keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… it feels fast, but I don’t know if I’ve ever really timed it or paid attention in that specific way.\n\nWhen it’s happening, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m eating quickly.” I’m more just kind of in it, you know? Like I’ll look down and a lot of the food is gone sooner than I expected, but I don’t always know if that means I was eating really fast or if I just wasn’t paying attention.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t want to say yes like I know for sure. I just know it can feel like everything kind of happens in a blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.\n\nMy clothes fit differently sometimes, or I’ll feel heavier or bloated after certain periods, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain over time or just how I’m feeling in my body. And I don’t really have a clear before-and-after number I can give you.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are eating more quickly than you normally would during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.\n\nCompared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food in response to feeling stressed, anxious, or upset after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically after work, or specifically because I’m stressed or anxious. I mean, work can definitely leave me feeling worn out sometimes, just from standing there and dealing with people all day, but I don’t know if I can honestly connect it in a straight line like, “I had a bad shift, so then I ate a large amount.”\n\nSometimes by the time I get home, I’m just kind of drained and not really thinking clearly, and eating can sort of happen before I’ve even sorted out what I’m feeling. But I don’t always know if I’m upset first, or if I’m just tired, or if it’s more like I’m alone and the day is over and there’s nothing else I have to focus on.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say usually, no. Not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. Once I start, it feels like I can’t really stop, even when part of me is trying to.\n\nIt’s like once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off. I’ll be thinking, like, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough.” I’ll actually be saying that in my head, not just afterward but while it’s happening. And then my hands still keep reaching for the next thing anyway, like I’m watching myself do it but not really controlling it.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I want more food now.” It feels more automatic than that. I can know I should stop and still keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty immediately after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “immediately” every time. That word makes me hesitate, because sometimes right after it happens I’m kind of numb, like I’m just sitting there and realizing what I did, but I’m not even fully feeling it yet. It’s more like this blank feeling at first, like… okay, that just happened.\n\nThen the guilt can come in after, but I’m not sure exactly how fast. Sometimes it’s pretty soon, like once I see the wrappers or the empty containers or I notice how much is gone, then I start feeling really bad about myself. But other times I think I’m more dazed than guilty at first, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I can’t honestly say I usually feel guilty immediately. I do feel bad afterward a lot of the time, but the timing is kind of messy. It’s not always like the second I stop eating I’m hit with guilt. Sometimes it takes a little while for my head to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the clearest way to describe it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, it’s that once I’ve started, it feels like I don’t have control of it anymore.\n\nLike, once I take that first bite, something in me kind of clicks off. I can still hear myself thinking, you know, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a specific “make up for it” thing in that way. Like, I know what you mean with purging or laxatives or exercising really hard afterward, but… I don’t know. That’s not something I’ve really noticed as part of what happens after.

Usually after one of those episodes I’m more just sitting with the aftermath, if that makes sense. I don’t have a clear routine where I go, “Okay, now I have to do this to undo it.” I might have thoughts about wanting it not to have happened, but as far as those specific things you named, I don’t have a solid yes to give you.

I guess if you’re asking whether I regularly throw up, take laxatives, or go exercise for hours because of it, I don’t think so. At least not in a way that I’d recognize as a pattern.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I guess I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially after work. I’m a cashier, so after a long shift I’ll leave and instead of just going home, I’ll stop and buy these huge takeout orders. Like, not just a meal — it’ll be enough for a whole family, honestly. Burgers, fries, chicken, desserts, sometimes a mix from different places if I’ve got it in my head.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there and eat almost all of it by myself before I go home. It feels embarrassing to even say it out loud, because it’s just… a lot of food. Way more than I think one person should be eating in one sitting. I keep telling myself I’m not going to do it again, but after my shifts it keeps happening, and that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control while eating these large amounts of food after work?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I guess the honest answer is no, I don’t feel in control. It’s like I might be thinking clearly right before, like, “Okay, just eat some of it, don’t go crazy,” but once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already ate enough.” Like I’m actually saying it in my head, sometimes over and over. But my hands just keep going back for the next thing — another fry, another piece of chicken, opening another container, taking another bite — and it feels like I’m watching myself do it but not really stopping it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. At first maybe, but then it’s more like I’m just… locked into it. I keep eating even when that part of me knows I should stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I feel bad afterward, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes it’s more like I’m just sitting there with all the wrappers and containers around me and I feel kind of… blank, or embarrassed, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t always have a clear thought like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this heavy feeling, like I don’t want anybody to know what just happened.\n\nSo yeah, maybe guilt is part of it, but I guess I don’t really have a clean answer. It’s not something I’ve put words to very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a point during these episodes where the food doesn't even taste good to you anymore, but you continue eating anyway?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because it’s not even like I’m still eating because it tastes amazing the whole time.\n\nAt the beginning, sure, it’s like I really want it, and the first bites feel like this relief almost. But then after a while, I’m not even really tasting it the same way. It’s just chewing and reaching and opening the next container. I’ll have this thought in my head like, Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough. And I really do mean it when I think that.\n\nBut once I’ve started, once I take that first bite, it feels like something in me clicks off. Like the part of me that can make a normal decision just kind of… shuts down. My hands keep moving before I can stop them. I’ll be telling myself to stop while I’m still grabbing another fry or another piece of food. So yeah, even when it’s not tasting good anymore, I keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… it feels fast, but I don’t know if I’ve ever really timed it or paid attention in that specific way.\n\nWhen it’s happening, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m eating quickly.” I’m more just kind of in it, you know? Like I’ll look down and a lot of the food is gone sooner than I expected, but I don’t always know if that means I was eating really fast or if I just wasn’t paying attention.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t want to say yes like I know for sure. I just know it can feel like everything kind of happens in a blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.\n\nMy clothes fit differently sometimes, or I’ll feel heavier or bloated after certain periods, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain over time or just how I’m feeling in my body. And I don’t really have a clear before-and-after number I can give you.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are eating more quickly than you normally would during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.\n\nCompared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food in response to feeling stressed, anxious, or upset after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically after work, or specifically because I’m stressed or anxious. I mean, work can definitely leave me feeling worn out sometimes, just from standing there and dealing with people all day, but I don’t know if I can honestly connect it in a straight line like, “I had a bad shift, so then I ate a large amount.”\n\nSometimes by the time I get home, I’m just kind of drained and not really thinking clearly, and eating can sort of happen before I’ve even sorted out what I’m feeling. But I don’t always know if I’m upset first, or if I’m just tired, or if it’s more like I’m alone and the day is over and there’s nothing else I have to focus on.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say usually, no. Not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. It might happen around those feelings sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say it’s mostly stress or anxiety after work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I can say more clearly. Once I start, it feels like I can’t really stop, even when part of me is trying to.\n\nIt’s like once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off. I’ll be thinking, like, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough.” I’ll actually be saying that in my head, not just afterward but while it’s happening. And then my hands still keep reaching for the next thing anyway, like I’m watching myself do it but not really controlling it.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I want more food now.” It feels more automatic than that. I can know I should stop and still keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty immediately after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “immediately” every time. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I guess I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially after work. I’m a cashier, so after a long shift I’ll leave and instead of just going home, I’ll stop and buy these huge takeout orders. Like, not just a meal — it’ll be enough for a whole family, honestly. Burgers, fries, chicken, desserts, sometimes a mix from different places if I’ve got it in my head.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there and eat almost all of it by myself before I go home. It feels embarrassing to even say it out loud, because it’s just… a lot of food. Way more than I think one person should be eating in one sitting. I keep telling myself I’m not going to do it again, but after my shifts it keeps happening, and that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control while eating these large amounts of food after work?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I guess the honest answer is no, I don’t feel in control. It’s like I might be thinking clearly right before, like, “Okay, just eat some of it, don’t go crazy,” but once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already ate enough.” Like I’m actually saying it in my head, sometimes over and over. But my hands just keep going back for the next thing — another fry, another piece of chicken, opening another container, taking another bite — and it feels like I’m watching myself do it but not really stopping it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. At first maybe, but then it’s more like I’m just… locked into it. I keep eating even when that part of me knows I should stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I feel bad afterward, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes it’s more like I’m just sitting there with all the wrappers and containers around me and I feel kind of… blank, or embarrassed, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t always have a clear thought like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this heavy feeling, like I don’t want anybody to know what just happened.\n\nSo yeah, maybe guilt is part of it, but I guess I don’t really have a clean answer. It’s not something I’ve put words to very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a point during these episodes where the food doesn't even taste good to you anymore, but you continue eating anyway?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because it’s not even like I’m still eating because it tastes amazing the whole time.\n\nAt the beginning, sure, it’s like I really want it, and the first bites feel like this relief almost. But then after a while, I’m not even really tasting it the same way. It’s just chewing and reaching and opening the next container. I’ll have this thought in my head like, Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough. And I really do mean it when I think that.\n\nBut once I’ve started, once I take that first bite, it feels like something in me clicks off. Like the part of me that can make a normal decision just kind of… shuts down. My hands keep moving before I can stop them. I’ll be telling myself to stop while I’m still grabbing another fry or another piece of food. So yeah, even when it’s not tasting good anymore, I keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… it feels fast, but I don’t know if I’ve ever really timed it or paid attention in that specific way.\n\nWhen it’s happening, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m eating quickly.” I’m more just kind of in it, you know? Like I’ll look down and a lot of the food is gone sooner than I expected, but I don’t always know if that means I was eating really fast or if I just wasn’t paying attention.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t want to say yes like I know for sure. I just know it can feel like everything kind of happens in a blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.\n\nMy clothes fit differently sometimes, or I’ll feel heavier or bloated after certain periods, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain over time or just how I’m feeling in my body. And I don’t really have a clear before-and-after number I can give you.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are eating more quickly than you normally would during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.\n\nCompared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food in response to feeling stressed, anxious, or upset after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically after work, or specifically because I’m stressed or anxious. I mean, work can definitely leave me feeling worn out sometimes, just from standing there and dealing with people all day, but I don’t know if I can honestly connect it in a straight line like, “I had a bad shift, so then I ate a large amount.”\n\nSometimes by the time I get home, I’m just kind of drained and not really thinking clearly, and eating can sort of happen before I’ve even sorted out what I’m feeling. But I don’t always know if I’m upset first, or if I’m just tired, or if it’s more like I’m alone and the day is over and there’s nothing else I have to focus on.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say usually, no. Not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. It might happen around those feelings sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say it’s mostly stress or anxiety after work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I can say more clearly. Once I start, it feels like I can’t really stop, even when part of me is trying to.\n\nIt’s like once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off. I’ll be thinking, like, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough.” I’ll actually be saying that in my head, not just afterward but while it’s happening. And then my hands still keep reaching for the next thing anyway, like I’m watching myself do it but not really controlling it.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I want more food now.” It feels more automatic than that. I can know I should stop and still keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty immediately after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “immediately” every time. That word makes me hesitate, because sometimes right after it happens I’m kind of numb, like I’m just sitting there and realizing what I did, but I’m not even fully feeling it yet. It’s more like this blank feeling at first, like… okay, that just happened.\n\nThen the guilt can come in after, but I’m not sure exactly how fast. Sometimes it’s pretty soon, like once I see the wrappers or the empty containers or I notice how much is gone, then I start feeling really bad about myself. But other times I think I’m more dazed than guilty at first, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I can’t honestly say I usually feel guilty immediately. I do feel bad afterward a lot of the time, but the timing is kind of messy. It’s not always like the second I stop eating I’m hit with guilt. Sometimes it takes a little while for my head to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the clearest way to describe it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, it’s that once I’ve started, it feels like I don’t have control of it anymore.\n\nLike, once I take that first bite, something in me kind of clicks off. I can still hear myself thinking, you know, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. This is enough.” I’ll be telling myself that while it’s happening, not just afterward when I’m looking back on it. It doesn’t feel like a normal choice in that moment. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by purging, using laxatives, or excessively exercising afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a specific “make up for it” thing in that way. Like, I know what you mean with purging or laxatives or exercising really hard afterward, but… I don’t know. That’s not something I’ve really noticed as part of what happens after.\n\nUsually after one of those episodes I’m more just sitting with the aftermath, if that makes sense. I don’t have a clear routine where I go, “Okay, now I have to do this to undo it.” I might have thoughts about wanting it not to have happened, but as far as those specific things you named, I don’t have a solid yes to give you.\n\nI guess if you’re asking whether I regularly throw up, take laxatives, or go exercise for hours because of it, I don’t think so. At least not in a way that I’d recognize as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like making myself throw up on purpose after it happens. I’ve definitely felt sick or overly full afterward sometimes, like my stomach is uncomfortable and I’m sitting there regretting it, but I don’t go to the bathroom and force myself to vomit or anything like that.\n\nI guess I want to be careful how I answer, because there have been times I’ve thought, like, “I wish I could just undo this,” but that’s more a thought or a feeling of wanting it gone, not me actually doing that. I don’t have a pattern of making myself vomit after those episodes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, I don’t think so. Not in that way.

Like the part of me that can make a normal decision just kind of… shuts down. My hands keep moving before I can stop them. I’ll be telling myself to stop while I’m still grabbing another fry or another piece of food. So yeah, even when it’s not tasting good anymore, I keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… it feels fast, but I don’t know if I’ve ever really timed it or paid attention in that specific way.\n\nWhen it’s happening, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m eating quickly.” I’m more just kind of in it, you know? Like I’ll look down and a lot of the food is gone sooner than I expected, but I don’t always know if that means I was eating really fast or if I just wasn’t paying attention.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t want to say yes like I know for sure. I just know it can feel like everything kind of happens in a blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.\n\nMy clothes fit differently sometimes, or I’ll feel heavier or bloated after certain periods, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain over time or just how I’m feeling in my body. And I don’t really have a clear before-and-after number I can give you.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are eating more quickly than you normally would during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.\n\nCompared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food in response to feeling stressed, anxious, or upset after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically after work, or specifically because I’m stressed or anxious. I mean, work can definitely leave me feeling worn out sometimes, just from standing there and dealing with people all day, but I don’t know if I can honestly connect it in a straight line like, “I had a bad shift, so then I ate a large amount.”\n\nSometimes by the time I get home, I’m just kind of drained and not really thinking clearly, and eating can sort of happen before I’ve even sorted out what I’m feeling. But I don’t always know if I’m upset first, or if I’m just tired, or if it’s more like I’m alone and the day is over and there’s nothing else I have to focus on.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say usually, no. Not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. It might happen around those feelings sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say it’s mostly stress or anxiety after work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I can say more clearly. Once I start, it feels like I can’t really stop, even when part of me is trying to.\n\nIt’s like once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off. I’ll be thinking, like, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough.” I’ll actually be saying that in my head, not just afterward but while it’s happening. And then my hands still keep reaching for the next thing anyway, like I’m watching myself do it but not really controlling it.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I want more food now.” It feels more automatic than that. I can know I should stop and still keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty immediately after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “immediately” every time. That word makes me hesitate, because sometimes right after it happens I’m kind of numb, like I’m just sitting there and realizing what I did, but I’m not even fully feeling it yet. It’s more like this blank feeling at first, like… okay, that just happened.\n\nThen the guilt can come in after, but I’m not sure exactly how fast. Sometimes it’s pretty soon, like once I see the wrappers or the empty containers or I notice how much is gone, then I start feeling really bad about myself. But other times I think I’m more dazed than guilty at first, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I can’t honestly say I usually feel guilty immediately. I do feel bad afterward a lot of the time, but the timing is kind of messy. It’s not always like the second I stop eating I’m hit with guilt. Sometimes it takes a little while for my head to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the clearest way to describe it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, it’s that once I’ve started, it feels like I don’t have control of it anymore.\n\nLike, once I take that first bite, something in me kind of clicks off. I can still hear myself thinking, you know, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. This is enough.” I’ll be telling myself that while it’s happening, not just afterward when I’m looking back on it. But even with that thought going through my head, my hands just keep reaching for the next thing, and then the next thing.\n\nIt’s a really strange feeling, because part of me is there and knows I want to stop, but another part is just… doing it anyway. It doesn’t feel like a normal choice in that moment. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by purging, using laxatives, or excessively exercising afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a specific “make up for it” thing in that way. Like, I know what you mean with purging or laxatives or exercising really hard afterward, but… I don’t know. That’s not something I’ve really noticed as part of what happens after.\n\nUsually after one of those episodes I’m more just sitting with the aftermath, if that makes sense. I don’t have a clear routine where I go, “Okay, now I have to do this to undo it.” I might have thoughts about wanting it not to have happened, but as far as those specific things you named, I don’t have a solid yes to give you.\n\nI guess if you’re asking whether I regularly throw up, take laxatives, or go exercise for hours because of it, I don’t think so. At least not in a way that I’d recognize as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not like making myself throw up on purpose after it happens. I’ve definitely felt sick or overly full afterward sometimes, like my stomach is uncomfortable and I’m sitting there regretting it, but I don’t go to the bathroom and force myself to vomit or anything like that.\n\nI guess I want to be careful how I answer, because there have been times I’ve thought, like, “I wish I could just undo this,” but that’s more a thought or a feeling of wanting it gone, not me actually doing that. I don’t have a pattern of making myself vomit after those episodes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in that way.\n\nI mean, I know what laxatives are, obviously, and I understand why you’re asking, but after those episodes I don’t go take them to try to clear everything out or undo what I ate. That’s not really something I’ve done as a response to it.\n\nAfterward I’m usually more just uncomfortable and upset with myself, and kind of stuck in my head about what happened. But I’m not reaching for laxatives or planning around that or anything. So for that specific question, I’d say no, I don’t use laxatives after.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean… I’ve had moments where I think, “I should do something, I should go walk this off,” or I feel guilty and restless afterward, but I wouldn’t say I go into some intense workout or exercise for hours to try to cancel it out.

Most of the time after it happens, I honestly feel too heavy and uncomfortable to do much of anything. I might pace around a little or tell myself I should be more active tomorrow, but it’s not like I immediately go running or force myself through a workout because of what I ate.

So, no, I don’t think I exercise excessively after those episodes. It’s more that I sit with the guilt and discomfort than try to undo it that way.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I guess I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially after work. I’m a cashier, so after a long shift I’ll leave and instead of just going home, I’ll stop and buy these huge takeout orders. Like, not just a meal — it’ll be enough for a whole family, honestly. Burgers, fries, chicken, desserts, sometimes a mix from different places if I’ve got it in my head.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there and eat almost all of it by myself before I go home. It feels embarrassing to even say it out loud, because it’s just… a lot of food. Way more than I think one person should be eating in one sitting. I keep telling myself I’m not going to do it again, but after my shifts it keeps happening, and that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control while eating these large amounts of food after work?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I guess the honest answer is no, I don’t feel in control. It’s like I might be thinking clearly right before, like, “Okay, just eat some of it, don’t go crazy,” but once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off.\n\nAnd I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already ate enough.” Like I’m actually saying it in my head, sometimes over and over. But my hands just keep going back for the next thing — another fry, another piece of chicken, opening another container, taking another bite — and it feels like I’m watching myself do it but not really stopping it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. At first maybe, but then it’s more like I’m just… locked into it. I keep eating even when that part of me knows I should stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I feel bad afterward, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes it’s more like I’m just sitting there with all the wrappers and containers around me and I feel kind of… blank, or embarrassed, like, “Why did I do that again?” But I don’t always have a clear thought like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this heavy feeling, like I don’t want anybody to know what just happened.\n\nSo yeah, maybe guilt is part of it, but I guess I don’t really have a clean answer. It’s not something I’ve put words to very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a point during these episodes where the food doesn't even taste good to you anymore, but you continue eating anyway?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because it’s not even like I’m still eating because it tastes amazing the whole time.\n\nAt the beginning, sure, it’s like I really want it, and the first bites feel like this relief almost. But then after a while, I’m not even really tasting it the same way. It’s just chewing and reaching and opening the next container. I’ll have this thought in my head like, Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough. And I really do mean it when I think that.\n\nBut once I’ve started, once I take that first bite, it feels like something in me clicks off. Like the part of me that can make a normal decision just kind of… shuts down. My hands keep moving before I can stop them. I’ll be telling myself to stop while I’m still grabbing another fry or another piece of food. So yeah, even when it’s not tasting good anymore, I keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… it feels fast, but I don’t know if I’ve ever really timed it or paid attention in that specific way.\n\nWhen it’s happening, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m eating quickly.” I’m more just kind of in it, you know? Like I’ll look down and a lot of the food is gone sooner than I expected, but I don’t always know if that means I was eating really fast or if I just wasn’t paying attention.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t want to say yes like I know for sure. I just know it can feel like everything kind of happens in a blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I know that probably sounds like something I should know, but I don’t weigh myself regularly. I kind of avoid it, actually, because I don’t want to see a number and then have that stuck in my head all day.\n\nMy clothes fit differently sometimes, or I’ll feel heavier or bloated after certain periods, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain over time or just how I’m feeling in my body. And I don’t really have a clear before-and-after number I can give you.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the eating. I just know I worry about it, and I notice my body, but I don’t have a concrete answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are eating more quickly than you normally would during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very clean answer, but I don’t pay attention to the speed in the moment. It’s more like… I look up and the food is just gone, or way more of it is gone than I expected, and then I’m trying to piece together what happened.\n\nCompared to how I normally eat, I guess it might be faster? But I don’t want to say that like it’s a definite thing, because when I’m eating normally I’m aware of it — like I’m taking bites, I’m thinking about the food, I’m talking if I’m with someone. During those episodes, I’m not really tracking it that way. It’s kind of automatic, so it’s hard for me to compare.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve clearly noticed, “Yes, I’m eating faster than usual.” It’s more that the whole thing feels blurred and out of my control than that I’m specifically aware of speed.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food in response to feeling stressed, anxious, or upset after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically after work, or specifically because I’m stressed or anxious. I mean, work can definitely leave me feeling worn out sometimes, just from standing there and dealing with people all day, but I don’t know if I can honestly connect it in a straight line like, “I had a bad shift, so then I ate a large amount.”\n\nSometimes by the time I get home, I’m just kind of drained and not really thinking clearly, and eating can sort of happen before I’ve even sorted out what I’m feeling. But I don’t always know if I’m upset first, or if I’m just tired, or if it’s more like I’m alone and the day is over and there’s nothing else I have to focus on.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say usually, no. Not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. It might happen around those feelings sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say it’s mostly stress or anxiety after work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I can say more clearly. Once I start, it feels like I can’t really stop, even when part of me is trying to.\n\nIt’s like once I take that first bite, something in me just kind of clicks off. I’ll be thinking, like, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. You already had enough.” I’ll actually be saying that in my head, not just afterward but while it’s happening. And then my hands still keep reaching for the next thing anyway, like I’m watching myself do it but not really controlling it.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I want more food now.” It feels more automatic than that. I can know I should stop and still keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty immediately after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “immediately” every time. That word makes me hesitate, because sometimes right after it happens I’m kind of numb, like I’m just sitting there and realizing what I did, but I’m not even fully feeling it yet. It’s more like this blank feeling at first, like… okay, that just happened.\n\nThen the guilt can come in after, but I’m not sure exactly how fast. Sometimes it’s pretty soon, like once I see the wrappers or the empty containers or I notice how much is gone, then I start feeling really bad about myself. But other times I think I’m more dazed than guilty at first, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I can’t honestly say I usually feel guilty immediately. I do feel bad afterward a lot of the time, but the timing is kind of messy. It’s not always like the second I stop eating I’m hit with guilt. Sometimes it takes a little while for my head to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the clearest way to describe it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m eating more than I meant to, it’s that once I’ve started, it feels like I don’t have control of it anymore.\n\nLike, once I take that first bite, something in me kind of clicks off. I can still hear myself thinking, you know, “Demetrius, stop. You don’t need any more. This is enough.” I’ll be telling myself that while it’s happening, not just afterward when I’m looking back on it. But even with that thought going through my head, my hands just keep reaching for the next thing, and then the next thing.\n\nIt’s a really strange feeling, because part of me is there and knows I want to stop, but another part is just… doing it anyway. It doesn’t feel like a normal choice in that moment. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by purging, using laxatives, or excessively exercising afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a specific “make up for it” thing in that way. That’s not something I’ve really noticed as part of what happens after.\n\nUsually after one of those episodes I’m more just sitting with the aftermath, if that makes sense. I don’t have a clear routine where I go, “Okay, now I have to do this to undo it.” I might have thoughts about wanting it not to have happened, but as far as those specific things you named, I don’t have a solid yes to give you.\n\nI guess if you’re asking whether I regularly throw up, take laxatives, or go exercise for hours because of it, I don’t think so. At least not in a way that I’d recognize as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I don’t have a pattern of making myself vomit after those episodes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in that way.\n\nI mean, I know what laxatives are, obviously, and I understand why you’re asking, but after those episodes I don’t go take them to try to clear everything out or undo what I ate.
